7.
Stable normal trivializations

In this section we confirm that all of the situations in this paper to which we apply Seidel-Smith localization in fact satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem
2.6
and Corollary
2.7
. In all cases the symplectic hypotheses are trivial; our symplectic manifolds are exact and convex at infinity, our Lagrangians are products of spheres and therefore exact, and after possibly averaging the symplectic forms appropriately, the involutions we consider are symplectic. It remains to check the algebraic-topological hypotheses of Seidel-Smith localization, namely that for each instance of a triple
(M,\EuScript{L}_{0},\EuScript{L}_{1})
with a symplectic involution
\iota
preserving the Lagrangians setwise, the fixed data
(M^{fix},\EuScript{L}^{fix},\EuScript{L}_{1}^{fix})
admits a stable normal trivialization.

Our proof is essentially the same as Seidel and Smith’s proof for the situations of their spectral sequences (
4.1
) from symplectic Khovanov homology to the Heegaard Floer homology of the branched double cover of the mirror of the link, and (
1.2
) from the symplectic Khovanov homology of a two-periodic link to that of its quotient
[
SS10
, Lemma 31]
. We first collect some basic definitions.

Definition 7.1

(Stable trivialization)
.

A complex (or real) vector bundle
V
over a manifold
M
is said to be stably trivial if there exists an isomorphism
\phi\colon V\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
(respectively
\phi\colon V\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
) for some values of
k
and
n
. In the complex case this is equivalent to the existence of a short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

and similarly in the real case mutatis mutandis. In particular, the choice of a stable trivialization
\phi
is equivalent to a choice of splitting of the exact sequence above.

Definition 7.2

(Relative stable trivialization with respect to Lagrangian subbundles)
.

Let
M
be a symplectic manifold and
\EuScript{L}
be a Lagrangian submanifold in
M
. We say that the pair
(TM,T\EuScript{L})
is relatively stably trivial if

•

TM
has a complex stable trivialization
\phi\colon TM\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}

•

T\EuScript{L}
is stably trivial as a real vector bundle

•

If
\Upsilon(M)\rightarrow M\times[0,1]
is the pullback of
TM
to
M\times[0,1]
, there is a Lagrangian subbundle
\Lambda\subset(\Upsilon(TM)\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k})|_{\EuScript{L}\times[0,1]}
such that
\Lambda|_{\{0\}\times\EuScript{L}}=T\EuScript{L}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
and
\phi(\Lambda|_{\{1\}\times\EuScript{L}})=\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

The pair
(TM,T\EuScript{L})
is relatively stably trivial if there exists a complex stable trivialization of the tangent bundle
TM
which restricts to a real stable trivialization of the tangent bundle
T\EuScript{L}
. This is equivalent to the existence of a complex stable trivialization of
TM
given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

and a commutative diagram of complex vector bundles over the Lagrangian
\EuScript{L}
as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\EuScript{L}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\underline{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

where the first vertical isomorphism is
v\otimes 1\mapsto v,\;v\otimes i\mapsto Jv
and the other vertical isomorphisms are the canonical maps given by
v\otimes 1\mapsto v,\;v\otimes i\mapsto iv
.

Proof.

The first assertion, nearly trivial, is proved by the first author in
[
Hen12
, Lemma 7.1]
. We prove the second assertion by unraveling definitions. Suppose first that there is a complex stable trivialization
\Phi\colon TM\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
which restricts to a real stable trivialization of
T\EuScript{L}
given by
\phi\colon T\EuScript{L}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then, we can choose the maps in the second row to be
F(v)=\Phi(v,0)
and
G(v)=pr_{\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}}\circ\Phi^{-1}(v)
, where
pr_{\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}}
denotes projection onto the
\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}
term. Similarly, we choose the maps in the first row to be
f(v\otimes z)=\phi(v,0)\otimes z
and
g(v\otimes z)=\left(pr_{\mathbb{C}^{k}}\circ\phi^{-1}(v)\right)\otimes z
. The rows are exact as
\Phi
and
\phi
are vector bundle isomorphisms. The squares in the diagram commute by construction since
\Phi
is a complex vector bundle morphism and therefore
\Phi(Jv)=i\phi(v)
and
\Phi^{-1}(iv)=i\phi^{-1}(v)
.

Now suppose that we have a complex stable trivialization of
TM
given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

and a commutative diagram of vector bundles over
\EuScript{L}
as in the statement of the lemma. Then, we can choose a splitting
S\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}\to\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
of the exact sequence above such that
G\circ S=id_{\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}}
. We lift this splitting
S
using the vertical isomorphisms to get a splitting
s\colon\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\otimes\underline{\mathbb{C}}\to\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\otimes\underline{\mathbb{C}}
. Then, we define
\Phi\colon TM\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}\to\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
by
\Phi(v,w)=F(v)+S(w)
. The map
\Phi
is then an isomorphism of complex vector bundles which gives the complex stable trivialization of
TM
. Similarly, we can define
\phi\colon T\EuScript{L}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\to\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
by
\phi(v,w)=f(v)+s(w)
, and
\phi
gives a real stable trivialization of
T\EuScript{L}
. By definition of the vertical maps, we see that
\phi
is the restriction of
\Phi
to the Lagrangian
\EuScript{L}
, so we have shown that
(TM,T\EuScript{L})
is relatively stably trivial.
∎

We now study the tangent bundles of manifolds that arise as the regular fiber of a smooth map.

Lemma 7.4
.

If
\pi\colon\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{k}
is a smooth map and
a\in\mathbb{C}^{k}
is a regular value of
\pi
, then the tangent bundle of the fibre
M_{a}=\pi^{-1}(a)
is stably trivial.

Proof.

By the regular value theorem, we know that
M_{a}
is a manifold. Let
j\colon M_{a}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
be the inclusion of the manifold as the fibre
\pi^{-1}(a)
. Then we have a short exact sequence of vector bundles on
M_{a}

(7.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\mathbb{C}^{n}|_{M_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{a}\times T_{a}\mathbb{C}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces dj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{a}\times d\pi}

where
p_{a}
is the projection map
p_{a}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muT\mathbb{C}^{n}|_{M_{a}}\rightarrow M_{a}
. Thus we see that
TM_{a}
is stably trivial.
∎

We derive as a corollary that the tangent bundle of
\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau}=\operatorname{Hilb}^{n,hor}(A_{\tau})
used in the computation of symplectic and annular symplectic Khovanov homology has stably trivial tangent bundle.

Corollary 7.5
.

The manifold
\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau}=\operatorname{Hilb}^{n,hor}(A_{\tau})
has stably trivial tangent bundle. The submanifold
\operatorname{Hilb}^{n,hor}(A_{\tau}^{*})
therefore does as well.

Proof.

The symplectic manifold
\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau}
is a regular fibre of the adjoint quotient map
\chi\colon S_{n}\to\operatorname{Sym}^{2n}(\mathbb{C})
over the point
\tau\in\operatorname{Conf}^{2n}_{0}(\mathbb{C})\subseteq\operatorname{Sym}^{2n}(\mathbb{C})
. By choosing local coordinates at
\tau
and recalling that
S_{n}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{4n}
, we can assume that we are in the set up of the Lemma
7.4
, from which the conclusions follow.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\pi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{C}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{k}
be a symplectic fibration, and let
\pi^{-1}(a)=M_{a}
for a regular value
a
. Let
t\mapsto a_{t}
for
t\in[0,1]
be a loop of regular values of
\pi
in
\mathbb{C}^{k}
, so that
a_{0}=a=a_{1}
. Consider a path of monodromy symplectomorphisms
\phi_{t}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muM_{a}\rightarrow M_{a_{t}}
such that
\phi=\phi_{1}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muM_{a}\rightarrow M_{a_{1}}=M_{a}
. Suppose that
\EuScript{L}
is a Lagrangian in
M
such that the trivialization of
TM
from the short exact sequence (
7.1
) restricts to a real stable trivialization of
TL
. Then the triple
(TM,T\EuScript{L},T(\phi(\EuScript{L})))
has a stable normal trivialization in the sense of Definition
2.5
.

Proof.

Consider the short exact sequence (
7.1
), replicated below with the additional notation of letting
j_{a}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muM_{a}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
be the inclusion map.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\mathbb{C}^{n}|_{M_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{a}\times T_{a}\mathbb{C}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d(j_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{a}\times d\pi}

We observe that there is a unique choice of splitting
s_{a}
which has
s_{a}(p_{a}\times d\pi)=\operatorname{id}
and sends
T_{a}(\mathbb{C}^{k})
to the orthogonal complement of
TM_{a}
in
T\mathbb{C}^{n}|_{M_{a}}
at each point. Any other choice is homotopic to this
s_{a}
via applying the Gram-Schmidt algorithm. This implies that using this preferred choice of splitting for each
t
, there is a continuously varying family of stable trivializations
\psi_{t}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muTM_{a_{t}}\times\mathbb{C}^{k}\rightarrow M_{a_{t}}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
given by
d(j_{a_{t}})\times s_{a_{t}}
with the property that
\psi_{0}=\psi_{1}=\psi
. Now let
p_{\mathbb{C}}
denote the projection from
M_{a_{t}}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
to
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. For
(v,x)\in T(\phi(\EuScript{L}))\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\subseteq TM_{a}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{C}}^{k}
, consider the homotopy

H_{t}(v,x)=p_{\mathbb{C}}(\psi_{1-t}((\phi_{1-t})_{*}(v,x)))

Then
H_{t}
is a homotopy of Lagrangian vector bundles between
\psi(T(\phi\EuScript{L})\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k})
and
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, which may be rotated to a homotopy between
\psi(T(\phi\EuScript{L})\oplus i\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k})
and
i\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
. As
\psi
also restricts to a real stable trivialization on
T\EuScript{L}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
we are done.
∎

We next show the tangent polarization data
(T\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau},T\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}},T(\beta\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}}))
on the triple
(\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau},\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}},\beta\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}})
is also stably trivial, where as in Section
3
,
\underline{c}
is a crossingless matching,
\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}}
is its associated Lagrangian, and the Lagrangian
\beta\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}}
is obtained by applying the symplectic monodromy map associated to the braid
\beta
. We begin by recalling the following from
[
SS10
, Lemma 29]
and
[
SS06
, Lemma 32]
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\mu_{1}<\ldots<\mu_{n}
be a collection of real numbers such that
\mu_{i}\neq 0
and
\{\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n}\}
is invariant under
z\mapsto-z
. For
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, consider the polydisc neighborhood
D_{\epsilon}\subseteq\operatorname{Conf}^{2n}_{0}(\mathbb{C})\subseteq\operatorname{Sym}^{2n}(\mathbb{C})
consisting of configurations

[\mu_{1}+\sqrt{z_{1}},\mu_{1}-\sqrt{z_{1}},\ldots,\mu_{n}+\sqrt{z_{n}},\mu_{n}-\sqrt{z_{n}}]

where
|z_{i}|<\epsilon
. For
\delta<\epsilon
, let
\underline{c}_{inv}
be the crossingless matching whose image is the set of intervals
[\mu_{i}-\sqrt{\delta},\mu_{i}+\sqrt{\delta}]
on the real line. Then
\chi^{-1}(D_{\epsilon})
contains a properly embedded contractible totally real submanifold
\Delta\subset\chi^{-1}(D_{\epsilon})
such that
\Delta\cap\chi^{-1}(\delta,\dots,\delta)
is Lagrangian isotopic to
\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}_{inv}}
in
\chi^{-1}(\delta,\dots,\delta)
.

Proof.

The fibre of
\chi
over
\{0,\ldots,0\}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2n}(\mathbb{C})
is singular and corresponds to degenerating the
2n
eigenvalues in pairs. From
[
SS06
, Lemma 28]
, we know that there are holomorphic local coordinates
(a_{1},b_{1},c_{1},\ldots,a_{n},b_{n},c_{n})
in
\chi^{-1}(D_{\epsilon})
centered around this singular fibre with respect to which the adjoint quotient
\chi
has the form

(7.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi^{-1}(D_{\epsilon})\cap S_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{r=1}^{n}\mathbb{C}^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\epsilon}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a_{1}^{2}+b_{1}^{2}+c_{1}^{2},\dots,a_{n}^{2}+b_{n}^{2}+c_{n}^{2})}

Let
\Delta
be the real locus in these coordinates, i.e.,
\Delta
is the fixed set of the complex conjugation of the complex structure. Seidel and Smith show that if
(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=(\delta,\dots,\delta)
in the disc
D_{\epsilon}
then the intersection
\EuScript{L}=\Delta\cap\chi^{-1}(\delta,\dots,\delta)
is Lagrangian isotopic in the fibre to
\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}_{inv}}

[
SS06
, Lemmas 32 and 36]
.
∎

Let the configuration
[\mu_{1}-\sqrt{\delta},\mu_{1}+\sqrt{\delta},\dots,\mu_{i}-\sqrt{\delta},\mu_{i}+\sqrt{\delta}]
be
\tau_{\delta}
.

Lemma 7.8
.

[
SS10
, Lemma 31]

The relative vector bundle
(T\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau_{\delta}},T\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}_{inv}})
is relatively stably trivial for the crossingless matching
\underline{c}_{inv}
. Moreover, the same is true of
(T\operatorname{Hilb}^{n,hor}(A_{\tau_{\delta}}),\Sigma_{\underline{c}_{inv}})
, where
\Sigma_{\underline{c}_{inv}}
denotes the Manolescu product Lagrangian.

Proof.

Consider the point
(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=(\delta,\dots,\delta)
in the disc
D_{\epsilon}
and the intersection
\EuScript{L}=\Delta\cap\chi^{-1}(\delta,\dots,\delta)
. We obtain a short exact sequence of real vector bundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\EuScript{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\Delta|_{\EuScript{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\EuScript{L}\times T_{\tau_{\delta}}(\operatorname{Conf}^{2n}_{0}(\mathbb{C}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces dj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times d\chi}

where
j
is the inclusion map
j\colon\EuScript{L}\to\Delta
and
\chi
is the restriction of the adjoint quotient map
\chi\colon\Delta\to D_{\epsilon}
. This shows that the tangent space
T\EuScript{L}
is stably trivial as a real vector bundle. Complexifying this short exact sequence, we obtain the following diagram.

(7.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\EuScript{L}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\Delta|_{\EuScript{L}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\EuScript{L}\times\mathbb{C}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\chi^{-1}(\delta,\ldots,\delta)|_{\EuScript{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\chi^{-1}(D_{\epsilon})|_{\EuScript{L}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\EuScript{L}\times\mathbb{C}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces dj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times d\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces dj}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times d\chi}

As
\EuScript{L}
is totally real in
\chi^{-1}(\delta,\ldots,\delta)
, we see that the complexification
T\EuScript{L}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
is isomorphic to
T\chi^{-1}(\delta,\ldots,\delta)|_{\EuScript{L}}
. The vertical isomorphisms are as described in Lemma
7.3
, and since the hypotheses of Lemma
7.3
are satisfied, we see that the relative vector bundle
(T\chi^{-1}(\delta,\ldots,\delta),T\EuScript{L})
is stably trivial.

Now, recall that
\EuScript{L}
is isotopic to
\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}_{inv}}
inside
\chi^{-1}(\delta,\ldots,\delta)
. Hence, the relative vector bundle
(T\chi^{-1}(\delta,\ldots,\delta),T\EuScript{L})
is carried by parallel transport to the relative bundle
(T\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau_{\delta}},T\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}_{inv}})
. This proves the first statement. Moreover,
L_{\underline{c}_{inv}}
is Lagrangian isotopic to the product Lagrangian
\Sigma_{\underline{c}_{inv}}
. Thus, we see that the relative vector bundle for the product Lagrangian
(T\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau},T\Sigma_{\underline{c}_{inv}})=(T\operatorname{Hilb}^{n,hor}(A_{\tau_{\delta}}),T\Sigma_{\underline{c}_{inv}})
is also stably trivial. ∎

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
{\underline{c}}
and
\underline{d}
be two crossingless matchings for the configuration
\tau_{\delta}
.
The tangent polarization
(T\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau_{\delta}},T\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}},T\EuScript{L}_{\underline{d}})
is stably trivial, as is the tangent polarization
(T\operatorname{Hilb}^{n,hor}(A_{\tau_{\delta}}),T\Sigma_{\underline{c}},T\Sigma_{\underline{d}})
.

Proof.

Up to symplectomorphism, we may take
\underline{c}=\underline{c}_{inv}
. From the previous lemma, we see that
(T\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau_{\delta}},TL_{\underline{c}_{inv}})
is stably trivial. Moreover,
\EuScript{L}_{\underline{d}}
can be obtained from
L_{\underline{c}_{inv}}
by symplectic parallel transport in the fibration
S_{n}\to\operatorname{Sym}^{2n}(\mathbb{C})
. Lemma
7.6
now implies that
(T\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau_{\delta}},T\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}},T\EuScript{L}_{\underline{d}})
is stably trivial. The second statement follows from the first via the usual isotopy of the Seidel-Smith vanishing cycle Lagrangian and the Manolescu product Lagrangian.
∎

We now turn our attention to equivariant trivializations. Consider a smooth vector bundle
\pi\colon V\to B
such that the total space
V
has a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
action
\iota\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muV\to V
which is an order-two fibrewise linear automorphism of
V
. Then
V
splits as
V\cong V^{inv}\oplus V^{anti}
where
V^{inv}
is the invariant subbundle of
V
preserved by the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
action, corresponding to the eigenvalue
+1
in each fibre, and
V^{anti}
is the anti-invariant subbundle of
V
, corresponding to the eigenvalue
-1
in each fibre.

Definition 7.10

(Equivariant stable trivialization)
.

We say that a stable trivialization of
V
is
\iota
-equivariant (or
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-equivariant) if there exists an order-two linear automorphism
T\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
which restricts to an order-two linear automorphism
T\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu\mathbb{C}^{k}\to\mathbb{C}^{k}
, with
k\leq n
, such that the following diagram, in which the two rows are identical and correspond to the stable trivialization, commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times\mathbb{C}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times\mathbb{C}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times\mathbb{C}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times\mathbb{C}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

Equivariant relative stable trivializations are defined similarly.

Lemma 7.11
.

If a smooth complex vector bundle
V\to B
has a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
equivariant stable trivialization, then there is an induced a stable trivialization of the invariant subbundle
V^{inv}
and anti-invariant subbundle
V^{anti}
. Similarly, if
V
is a complex vector bundle with a Lagrangian subbundle
W
, and the pair
(V,W)
has an
\iota
-equivariant relative stable trivialization, then this induces a relative stable trivialization of the invariant pair
(V^{inv},W^{inv})
and anti-invariant pair
(V^{anti},W^{anti})
.

Proof.

Consider the
\mathbb{Z}/2
equivariant stable trivialization for
V
with notation as in Definition
7.10
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times\mathbb{C}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times\mathbb{C}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times\mathbb{C}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times\mathbb{C}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\times T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

Since the involutions
\iota
and
id\times T
intertwine the inclusion map
\rho
and the quotient map
\xi
, it follows that the maps
\rho
and
\xi
must also split. In particular we have
\rho=\rho^{inv}\oplus\rho^{anti}
such that
\rho^{inv}\colon V^{inv}\to B\times(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{inv}
maps the invariant subbundle of
V
to the invariant subbundle of
B\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and
\rho^{anti}\colon V^{anti}\to B\times(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{anti}
maps one anti-invariant subbundle to the other. We similarly have
\xi=\xi^{inv}\oplus\xi^{anti}
. Then we see that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{inv}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{inv}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{inv}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{inv}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{inv}}

is a stable trivialization for the invariant subbundle
V^{inv}
and similarly

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{anti}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times(\mathbb{C}^{n})^{anti}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{anti}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{anti}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{anti}}

is a stable trivialization for
V^{anti}
. The statement for relative vector bundles is similar.
∎

We now apply this discussion to our situation. For notation, recall that we have considered three involutions on
\chi^{-1}(\tau)=\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau}
for
\tau\in\operatorname{Conf}^{2n}(\mathbb{C})\subseteq\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathbb{C})
in this paper, to wit

\iota_{A}(u,v,z)=(u,z,-z)\qquad\iota_{B}(u,v,z)=(-u,-v,-z)\qquad\iota_{D}(u,v,z)=(u,-v,z).

In the Appendix, we rephrase each of these in terms of the nilpotent slice coordinates on
\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau}
. With respect to this rephrasing, it follows that all three involutions have a natural extension to
S_{n}
. We observe that
\tau_{\delta}
and
\underline{c}_{inv}
have been chosen so that
\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau_{\delta}}
and
L_{\underline{c}_{inv}}
are preserved by these actions. We have the following mild extension of
[
SS10
, Lemma 29]
.

Lemma 7.12
.

The submanifold
\Delta\subseteq\chi^{-1}(D_{\epsilon})
of Lemma
7.7
is preserved by the natural extension of any of the three involutions induced on
\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau}
by
\iota_{A}
,
\iota_{B}
or
\iota_{D}
. Moreover, the isotopy between
\chi^{-1}(\delta,\dots,\delta)\cap\Delta
and
\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}_{inv}}
may be taken to be
\iota
-equivariant.

Proof.

As previously, let
\Delta
be the real locus in these coordinates given in (
7.2
) the
\chi^{-1}(D_{\epsilon})
so that,
\Delta
is the fixed set of the complex conjugation of the complex structure. Let
\iota
be the extension of any of the involutions on
Y_{n,\tau}
considered in this paper. As
\iota
preserves each fibre of
\chi
, it preserves
\chi^{-1}(D_{\epsilon})
. Moreover, as
\iota
is a symplectic involution, it preserves the complex structure and hence the real locus of the complex structure in this neighborhood. Thus, the neighborhood
\Delta
constructed above is preserved by
\iota
. The second assertion follows immediately from the construction of the isotopy in
[
SS06
, Lemma 32]
.∎

Moreover, the isotopy between the vanishing cycle Lagrangians and Manolescu’s product Lagrangians
[
Man06
, Lemmas 4.2 and 4.3]
may be made equivariant with respect to any of
\iota_{A}
,
\iota_{B}
, or
\iota_{D}
as follows.

Lemma 7.13
.

The Lagrangian isotopy relating the Seidel-Smith Lagrangian
\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}_{inv}}
and the Manolescu product Lagrangian
\Sigma_{\underline{c}_{inv}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Sigma_{c}
associated to the crossingless matching
\underline{c}
is equivariant with respect to any of the three involutions
\iota_{A}
,
\iota_{B}
and
\iota_{D}
.

Proof.

In the Appendix, we translate each of the three involutions into coordinates on the nilpotent slice. With
respect to this description, the Seidel-Smith Kähler form
\omega
on
S_{n}
is
\iota
-equivariant for
\iota
any of
\iota_{A}
,
\iota_{B}
, and
\iota_{D}
. Meanwhile, Manolescu constructs an extension of the form
\omega_{\mathcal{Y}}
to a form
\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}

S_{n}
which agrees with the product form induced by
\omega_{A_{\tau}}
on each fibre on a large compact set, which contains an open subset
U
of the two Lagrangians which itself lies inside the set

\mathcal{V}_{m,\tau}=\{[(u_{i},v_{i},z_{i})]\in\operatorname{Conf}^{2n}(A_{\tau}):z_{i}\neq z_{j}\text{ for all }i\neq j\}.

This form is clearly
\iota
-equivariant on
U
and may be made universally so via averaging. In
[
Man06
, Lemma 4.2]
, Manolescu interpolates linearly between these two symplectic forms; this interpolation is
\iota
-equivariant, implying that the Lagrangians constructed by iterated parallel transport for both the symplectic forms are also
\iota
-equivariantly isotopic. In
[
Man06
, Lemma 4.3]
, Manolescu uses the torus action
\tau
on the fibration

\mathcal{V}_{m,\tau}\rightarrow\operatorname{Sym}^{2n}(\mathbb{C})

and associated moment map
f
on the restriction to
U
to show that the iterated vanishing cycle construction for
\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}}
yields a product Lagrangian
\Sigma_{\underline{c}}
. The involutions
\iota_{A}
and
\iota_{B}
commute with the torus action
\tau
and hence preserve the moment map
f
and the fibre
f^{-1}(0)
. More subtly, the involution
\iota_{D}((u,v,z)=(u,-v,z)
does not commute with the torus action. Nonetheless the moment map
f_{\tau}=-f_{\iota\circ\tau}
, and hence the fibre
f^{-1}(0)
is still preserved. Therefore the construction of the further isotopy of
[
Man06
, Lemma 4.3]
carries through
\iota
-equivariantly. In particular, in any step when the istopy class of a Lagrangian is necessarily that of a product Lagrangian, it is in particular equivariantly isotopic to a product Lagrangian preserved by
\iota
.∎

Now, let
\iota
be any of the three actions
\iota_{A}
,
\iota_{B}
, and
\iota_{D}
, and let
\underline{c}
and
\underline{d}
be any two crossingless matchings with endpoints on
\tau_{\delta}
.

Lemma 7.14
.

The restriction of the polarization data
(T\mathcal{Y}_{n},T\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}},T\EuScript{L}_{\underline{d}})
to
(\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau_{\delta}}^{\mathrm{fix}},\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}}^{fix},\EuScript{L}_{\underline{d}}^{fix})
has an
\iota
-equivariant stable trivialization, as does the restriction of the
(T\operatorname{Hilb}^{n,hor}(A_{\tau,\delta}),T(\Sigma_{\underline{c}}),T(\Sigma_{\underline{d}}))
polarization data to
(\operatorname{Hilb}^{n,hor}(A_{\tau,\delta})^{fix},\Sigma_{\underline{c}}^{fix},\Sigma_{\underline{d}}^{fix})
.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
7.11
that the polarization data
(T\mathcal{Y}_{n,\bar{\mu}},T\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}},T\EuScript{L}_{\underline{d}})
is stably trivial. Moreover, as the neighborhoods
\Delta
and
\chi^{-1}(D_{\epsilon})
constructed in Lemma
7.7
which realize the relative stable trivialization are
\iota
-equivariant, we see that
\iota
will intertwines the commutative diagram of equation
7.3
. Thus, the relative stable trivialization from Lemma
7.8
is
\iota
-equivariant. Lemma
7.13
implies that the same is true if we consider the product Lagrangians.
∎

Theorem 7.15
.

If
\iota
is any of the three actions
\iota_{A}
,
\iota_{B}
, and
\iota_{D}
, then polarization data
(\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau_{\delta}}^{\mathrm{fix}},\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}}^{fix},\EuScript{L}_{\underline{d}}^{fix})
admits a stable normal trivialization in the sense of Definition
2.5
, and likewise if we substitute the product Lagrangian triple
(\operatorname{Hilb}^{n,hor}(A_{\tau,\delta})^{fix},\Sigma_{\underline{c}}^{fix},\Sigma_{\underline{d}}^{fix})
.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
7.14
and an equivariant version of the arguments of Lemma
7.11
that the relative normal bundle
(N\mathcal{Y}_{n,\tau_{\delta}},N\EuScript{L}_{\underline{c}},N\EuScript{L}_{\underline{d}})
has a stable trivialization. By Lemma
7.13
the same holds if we instead use the product Lagrangians.
∎

Appendix A
Comparison of coordinates

We conclude by describing how the involutions

\iota_{A}(u,v,z)=(u,v,-z),\quad\iota_{B}(u,v,z)=(-u,-v,-z),\quad\iota_{D}(u,v,z)=(u,-v,z)

considered in this paper act on
\mathcal{Y}_{m,\tau}
in the original nilpotent slice picture of Seidel and Smith. As explained in Remark
1.8
, in the case of
\iota_{A}
and
\iota_{B}
this resolves an apparent asymmetry in the actions used to recover the symplectic analogs of the Stoffregen-Zhang and Lipshitz-Sarkar spectral sequences, which on the combinatorial side are extremely similar.

Recall from Section
3.1.2
that if
G=GL_{2m}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathfrak{g}
be its Lie algebra, we consider the nilpotent slice
S_{m}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}
which is the affine subspace consisting of matrices

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccccccccccccccccccccc}a_{1}&1&&&b_{1}&&&\\
a_{2}&0&1&&b_{2}&&&\\
\dots&&\dots&&\dots&&\\
a_{m-1}&&&1&b_{m-1}&&&\\
a_{m}&&&0&b_{m}&&&\\
c_{1}&&&&d_{1}&1&&\\
c_{2}&&&&d_{2}&0&1&\\
\dots&&&&\dots&&&\dots&1\\
c_{m}&&&&d_{m}&&&&0\end{array}\right)

and that for
\tau\in\operatorname{Conf}^{2m}(\mathbb{C})
, we define

(A.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{m,\tau}:=\chi^{-1}(\tau)

where
\chi
is the adjoint quotient map
\chi\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0muS_{m}\to\operatorname{Sym}^{2m}(\mathbb{C})
, which sends a matrix to its set of coefficients of its characteristic polynomial. We group the elements of this matrix into matrices
A_{i}\in\mathfrak{gl}_{2}{\mathbb{C}}
where

A_{i}=\left(\begin{matrix}a_{i}&b_{i}\\
c_{i}&d_{i}\end{matrix}\right).

In
[
SS10
, Section 4.1]
, Seidel and Smith describe the nilpotent slice in terms of these matrices, so that

A=\begin{pmatrix}&A_{1}&I&0&0&\cdots\\
&A_{2}&0&I&0&\cdots\\
&\cdots\\
&A_{m-1}&0&0&\cdots&I\\
&A_{m}&0&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}.

(In the original formulation, the condition
a_{1}=-d_{1}
is also imposed, with the result that we may insist that
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, but this difference is not important to what follows.)

Let

A(x)=x^{m}I-x^{m-1}A_{1}-x^{m-2}A_{2}-\cdots-A_{m}\in\mathfrak{gl}_{2}(\mathbb{C}[x])

so that

\det(xI-A)=\det(A(x))=(x-\tau_{1})\dots(x-\tau_{2m})=p(x).

The involution considered by Seidel and Smith for 2-periodic links in
[
SS10
, Section 5]
is as below, for
m
even.

\displaystyle\iota\colon\mathcal{Y}_{m,\tau}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Y}_{m,\tau}

\displaystyle(A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{m-1},A_{m})

\displaystyle\mapsto(-A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,-A_{m-1},A_{m})

For their case, this corresponds to the involution induced by
\iota_{A}
on the Hilbert scheme. We will now describe how this and the involution induced by
\iota_{B}
on the Hilbert scheme translate into the nilpotent slice picture more generally. For a matrix
A\in M_{2\times 2}(\mathbb{C})
, let

A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix},\;\;\;\widehat{A}=\begin{pmatrix}a&-b\\
-c&d\end{pmatrix}.

Lemma A.1
.

The involutions induced by
\iota_{A}
and
\iota_{B}
on the horizontal Hilbert scheme correspond to the following maps on the nilpotent slice.

(1)

When
m
is even,

\iota\colon(A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{m})\mapsto(-A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,-A_{m-1},A_{m})

corresponds to the involution induced by
\iota_{A}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu(u,v,z)\mapsto(u,v,-z)
.

(2)

When
m
is even

\iota^{\prime}\colon(A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{m})\mapsto(-\widehat{A}_{1},\widehat{A}_{2},\cdots,-\widehat{A}_{m-1},\widehat{A}_{m})

corresponds to the involution induced by
\iota_{B}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu(u,v,z)\mapsto(-u,-v,-z).

(3)

When
m=2k+1
is odd,

\iota\colon(A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{2k-1},A_{2k},A_{2k+1})\mapsto(-A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,-A_{2k-1},A_{2k},-A_{2k+1})

corresponds to the involution induced by
\iota_{B}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu(u,v,z)\mapsto(-u,-v,-z).

(4)

When
m=2k+1
is odd,

\iota^{\prime}\colon(A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{m})\mapsto(-\widehat{A}_{1},\widehat{A}_{2},\cdots,-\widehat{A}_{2k-1},\widehat{A}_{2k},-\widehat{A}_{2k+1})

corresponds to the involution induced by
\iota_{A}\penalty\mskip 2.0mu\mathpunct{}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu{:}\penalty\mskip 6.0mu plus 1.0mu(u,v,z)\mapsto(u,v,-z).

Proof.

We will sketch a proof of this lemma by understanding the change of coordinates between the Hilbert scheme picture and the nilpotent slice picture more clearly. We will work on
\mathcal{U}_{m}=\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(A_{\tau})\setminus\Delta
, which is open in
\operatorname{Hilb}^{m,hor}(A_{\tau})
. Note that any
\overrightarrow{\xi}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(A_{\tau})\setminus\Delta
is given by coordinates

\overrightarrow{\xi}=\left\{(u_{1},v_{1},z_{1}),\dots,(u_{m},v_{m},z_{m}):(u_{i},v_{i},z_{i})\neq(u_{j},v_{j},z_{j})\text{ for }i\neq j\right\}.

We claim that it is sufficient to show the correspondence of the involutions over the open set
\mathcal{U}_{m}
. For, if the change of coordinates map from the Seidel-Smith to the Hilbert scheme coordinates is
\Phi
, let two involutions in the Seidel-Smith and Hilbert scheme coordinates be
\iota_{SS}
and
\iota_{Man}
respectively. Since the involutions
\iota_{SS}
and
\iota_{Man}
are holomorphic maps, and the change of coordinates map
\Phi
is also holomorphic, then by the identity theorem for multiple complex variables, if
\iota_{Man}
agrees with
\iota_{SS}\circ\Phi
on the open set
\mathcal{U}_{n}
, then they must also agree on the entire complex manifold
\mathcal{Y}_{m,\tau}
.

In
[
Man06
, Section 2.1]
, the nilpotent slice is described using slightly different coordinates

\displaystyle A(t)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=t^{m}-a_{1}^{\prime}t^{m-1}+a_{2}^{\prime}t^{m-2}\cdots+(-1)^{m}a_{m}^{\prime}

\displaystyle B(t)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=b_{1}^{\prime}t^{m-1}-b_{2}^{\prime}t^{m-2}+\cdots+(-1)^{m}b_{m}^{\prime}

\displaystyle C(t)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=c_{1}^{\prime}t^{m-1}-c_{2}^{\prime}t^{m-2}+\cdots+(-1)^{m}c_{m}^{\prime}

\displaystyle D(t)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=t^{m}-d_{1}^{\prime}t^{m-1}+d_{2}^{\prime}t^{m-2}\cdots+(-1)^{m}d_{m}^{\prime}

For
\tau\in\operatorname{Conf}^{2m}(\mathbb{C})
, we have

\mathcal{Y}_{m,\tau}=\{(A,B,C,D)\mid(AD-BC)(z)=(z-\tau_{1})\cdots(z-\tau_{2m})=p(z)\}

(It is again the case that Manolescu imposes the slightly stricter condition
a_{1}=-d_{1}
.) Comparing this with the definition from
[
SS10
, Section 4]
, where
A_{k}^{ij}
is the
ij
-th entry of the matrix
A_{k}

\det(A(x))=\det\begin{pmatrix}x^{m}-A_{1}^{11}x^{m-1}-A_{2}^{11}x^{m-2}-\cdots-A_{m}^{11}&-A_{1}^{12}x^{m-1}-A_{2}^{12}x^{m-2}-\cdots-A_{m}^{12}\\
-A_{1}^{21}x^{m-1}-A_{2}^{21}x^{m-2}-\cdots+A_{m}^{21}&x^{m}-A_{1}^{22}x^{m-1}-A_{2}^{22}x^{m-2}-\cdots-A_{m}^{22}\end{pmatrix}

we can identify

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}^{\prime}&b_{1}^{\prime}\\
c_{1}^{\prime}&d_{1}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\;\;\;

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-a_{2}^{\prime}&-b_{2}^{\prime}\\
-c_{2}^{\prime}&-d_{2}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle A_{2k-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a_{2k-1}^{\prime}&b_{2k-1}^{\prime}\\
c_{2k-1}^{\prime}&d_{2k-1}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\;\;\;

\displaystyle A_{2k}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-a_{2k}^{\prime}&-b_{2k}^{\prime}\\
-c_{2k}^{\prime}&-d_{2k}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

From
[
Man06
, Remark 2.8]
we see that
\overrightarrow{\xi}=\{(u_{1},v_{1},z_{1}),\cdots,(u_{m},v_{m},z_{m})\}
is obtained from the polynomials
A(t),B(t),C(t),D(t)
as follows. The coordinates
z_{k}
are the roots of
A(t)
, and for each
k
we have

\displaystyle u_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(B+C)(z_{k})\vcentcolon=U(z_{k})

\displaystyle v_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{-1}}(B-C)(z_{k})\vcentcolon=V(z_{k})

Now, when
m
is even

\iota\colon(A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{m-1},A_{m})\mapsto(-A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,-A_{m-1},A_{m})

corresponds to

\displaystyle A(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto A(-t)

\displaystyle B(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto B(-t)\;\;

\displaystyle U(t)\mapsto U(-t)

\displaystyle C(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto C(-t)\;\;

\displaystyle V(t)\mapsto V(-t)

\displaystyle D(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto D(-t)

Thus,
\iota
maps
z_{k}
to
-z_{k}
. We see that

\displaystyle U(-(-z_{k}))=U(z_{k})=u_{k}

\displaystyle V(-(-z_{k}))=V(z_{k})=v_{k}

so
\iota
corresponds to the involution induced the Hilbert scheme by
(u,v,z)\mapsto(u,v,-z)
. Similarly, for
m
odd,
m=2k+1

\iota\colon(A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{2k-1},A_{2k},A_{2k+1})\mapsto(-A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,-A_{2k-1},A_{2k},-A_{2k+1})

corresponds to

\displaystyle A(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto-A(-t)

\displaystyle B(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto-B(-t)\;\;

\displaystyle U(t)\mapsto-U(-t)

\displaystyle C(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto-C(-t)\;\;

\displaystyle V(t)\mapsto-V(-t)

\displaystyle D(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto-D(-t)

which is the involution induced by
(u,v,z)\mapsto(-u,-v,-z)
on the Hilbert scheme. We can also check that when
m
is even

\iota^{\prime}\colon(A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{m})\mapsto(-\widehat{A}_{1},\widehat{A}_{2},\cdots,-\widehat{A}_{m-1},\widehat{A}_{m})

corresponds to

\displaystyle A(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto A(-t)

\displaystyle B(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto-B(-t)\;\;

\displaystyle U(t)\mapsto-U(-t)

\displaystyle C(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto-C(-t)\;\;

\displaystyle V(t)\mapsto-V(-t)

\displaystyle D(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto D(-t)

which is induced by the involution
(u,v,z)\mapsto(-u,-v,-z)
on the Hilbert scheme, and for
m=2k+1
odd,

\iota^{\prime}\colon(A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{m})\mapsto(-\widehat{A}_{1},\widehat{A}_{2},\cdots,-\widehat{A}_{2k-1},\widehat{A}_{2k},-\widehat{A}_{2k+1})

corresponds to

\displaystyle A(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto-A(-t)

\displaystyle B(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto B(-t)\;\;

\displaystyle U(t)\mapsto U(-t)

\displaystyle C(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto C(-t)\;\;

\displaystyle V(t)\mapsto V(-t)

\displaystyle D(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto-D(-t)

which is induced by the involution
(u,v,z)\mapsto(u,v,-z)
on the Hilbert scheme. ∎

We now consider the behavior of the final involution. For a matrix
A\in M_{2\times 2}(\mathbb{C})
, let
A^{\intercal}
denote the transpose of
A
.

Lemma A.2
.

[
SS10
, Section 4.1]

The involution
\iota_{D}
on
\operatorname{Hilb}^{m,hor}(A_{\tau})
induced by
(u,v,z)\mapsto(u,-v,z)
from Section
4
corresponds in the Seidel-Smith coordinates to the involution

(A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{m-1},A_{m})\mapsto(A_{1}^{\intercal},A_{2}^{\intercal},\cdots,A_{m-1}^{\intercal},A_{m}^{\intercal}).

Proof.

Going through the intermediate coordinates
A(t)
,
B(t)
,
C(t)
, and
D(t)
as in Lemma
A.1
, we see that

(A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{m-1},A_{m})\mapsto(A_{1}^{\intercal},A_{2}^{\intercal},\cdots,A_{m-1}^{\intercal},A_{m}^{\intercal})

corresponds to

\displaystyle A(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto A(t)

\displaystyle B(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto C(t)\;\;

\displaystyle U(t)\mapsto U(t)

\displaystyle C(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto B(t)\;\;

\displaystyle V(t)\mapsto-V(t)

\displaystyle D(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto D(t)

As before, from
[
Man06
, Remark 2.8]
,
\overrightarrow{\xi}=\{(u_{1},v_{1},z_{1}),\cdots,(u_{m},v_{m},z_{m})\}
is obtained from
A(t),B(t),U(t)
, and
V(t)
such that the elements
z_{k}
are the roots of
A(t)
and

\displaystyle u_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(B+C)(z_{k})\vcentcolon=U(z_{k})

\displaystyle v_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{-1}}(B-C)(z_{k})\vcentcolon=V(z_{k})

Thus we see that the involution defined above on the Seidel-Smith nilpotent slice is induced by the involution
(u,v,z)\mapsto(u,-v,z)
on the Hilbert scheme in the Manolescu reformulation. Again, we have only shown this correspondence for the open set
\mathcal{U}_{n}\subset\mathcal{Y}_{n}
, but this is sufficient as we are working with holomorphic involutions and a holomorphic change of coordinates, so the identity theorem applies.
∎
